It’s getting noticeable, and I figured I should talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early. In grade school I was always getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, talking over the teacher before I even realized I was doing it, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd directions — if the teacher was explaining an assignment, I’d already be halfway into it before she finished, then I’d have to redo it. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” which I guess was her way of saying I couldn’t just settle down like the other kids. She said that a lot, actually.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably. Like if my supervisor starts telling me what needs doing in the building, I’ll jump in before he’s finished because I think I already know where he’s going with it. Same thing with church staff when they’re giving instructions for an event.\n\nAnd sometimes I’m right, but sometimes I’m not, and then they have to stop and correct me. Which is embarrassing, you know? I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve waited for the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like blurting out answers or doing things without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that. Mostly it’s with instructions — like my supervisor will start telling me what needs to be done, or someone at church is explaining how they want something set up, and I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they’ve finished.\n\nSometimes I’ve got it right, but other times I’m off, and then they have to stop and correct me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. It’s just like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: What brings me in is I feel like I’ve got a motor humming under my skin whenever I’m supposed to sit still, like in staff meetings or church event briefings, and I end up shifting, standing, or finding some excuse to move around.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even when I’m trying to relax, like fishing with my wife or sitting through bingo, I catch myself tapping, talking too loud, messing with my tackle box or pencil, and it gets hard for me to just be quiet and enjoy it.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On my shifts I go from mopping to checking trash to straightening chairs without really stopping, and even at home I’ll start puttering in the garage or re-sorting tools instead of sitting down after work.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: When my supervisor or one of the church staff starts giving me instructions, I jump in with what I think they’re going to say before they finish, and sometimes I get it wrong and have to be corrected.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for the freight elevator, the supply closet key, or somebody else to decide what room needs cleaning next makes me antsy and short, and I’ll pace or push to get moving before it’s really my turn.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: I remember getting in trouble back in grade school for popping out of my seat, talking over the teacher, and rushing ahead before directions were finished; Which is embarrassing, you know? I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve waited for the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like blurting out answers or doing things without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that. Mostly it’s with instructions — like my supervisor will start telling me what needs to be done, or someone at church is explaining how they want something set up, and I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they’ve finished.\n\nSometimes I’ve got it right, but other times I’m off, and then they have to stop and correct me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. It’s just like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one exactly. I mean, I finish my work, generally… at least I think I do. If someone gives me a list, I’ll usually get through it.\n\nSometimes people say “follow through” and I start thinking about, like, big projects, and I don’t really have many of those at work — it’s more cleaning rooms, checking bathrooms, taking trash out, floors, that kind of thing. Actually the floor buffer, that thing can be touchy if it’s not sitting right, and we had one at the school years ago that would pull hard to the left—\n\nSorry, what I mean is… I haven’t really noticed not finishing tasks as a big problem. I don’t know if I’d call it organizing, exactly. My day’s pretty routine, you know — trash, bathrooms, floors, supplies, that sort of thing. I don’t sit down and make a plan for it like some people do.

Sometimes if the day changes, like there’s an event or somebody needs a room turned over, I kind of just start with whatever seems most urgent. Which… now that I say it, maybe that is organizing, I’m not sure. At church they’ll have these setup lists sometimes, and the folding tables all have to face a certain way, and there’s always that one closet where the chairs get jammed in wrong—

Sorry. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed daily organizing as a big problem, but I might not be thinking of it the way you mean.

Yeah, that’s actually one of the main things that brings me in. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like in a staff meeting or when they’re going over a church event setup, I feel like there’s a motor humming under my skin or something.

I’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or I’ll find a reason to go check on something, move a chair, refill coffee, whatever. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just being useful, but really I think I just need to move. Those briefings can go on, and the chairs at church are those metal folding ones, the kind that pinch if you sit wrong—

But yeah, sitting still or standing still, I get restless pretty quick.

Physically intrude… like jumping into people’s conversations or games? I don’t know that I do that a lot. I mean, sometimes if I’m nearby and I hear someone talking about something in the building — like a spill or a room needing chairs — I might step in because I think I can help.

Yeah… I probably do that more than I should. Like if my supervisor starts telling me something, or one of the church staff says, “Can you go check—” I’ll jump in and say, “The bathrooms?” or “The fellowship hall?” before they even finish.

Sometimes I’m right, because it’s usually the same kinds of things, you know — supplies, trash, a spill, chairs needing to be moved. But sometimes I guess wrong and they have to stop me and say, “No, let me finish,” which is… a little embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut them off. My brain just kind of gets ahead of their sentence.

Mood swings, though… I’m not sure I’d say strong ones. I haven’t really noticed big ups and downs like that. But that’s sort of part of my job, too.\n\nIf I’m cleaning and somebody walks by or there’s a noise, I might look over, but I don’t know if I’d call it distracted. The other day there was this squeaky door near the supply closet, and once you hear it you can’t unhear it, you know? I kept thinking someone should oil that hinge, and then I was looking for the oil can, except I think it got moved after the last maintenance guy used it—\n\nSorry. Your question was whether I’m easily distracted. I guess I haven’t really kept track of that. I don’t know that I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, like inserting yourself into their conversations or games?\n\nPatient: Physically intrude… like jumping into people’s conversations or games? I don’t know that I do that a lot. I mean, sometimes if I’m nearby and I hear someone talking about something in the building — like a spill or a room needing chairs — I might step in because I think I can help.\n\nBut if you mean like barging in or getting in people’s space, I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t play games much, unless you count the church picnic stuff, and that’s usually me hauling tables around or making sure the trash bags don’t tear, because those cheap ones split right down the side—\n\nSorry. I guess my answer is I’m not sure that’s something I do, at least not that I’ve been told.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers to questions before the person has finished asking them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do that more than I should. I’m not real sure. I mean, if somebody’s in the middle of something and I need to ask where a mop bucket went, I might step in for a second, but I don’t know if that counts as interrupting.

At work people are always doing something, so it’s kind of hard to tell. Like if maintenance is working on a door and I need to get through with the floor machine, I’ll ask, or I’ll wave at them. That floor machine’s loud too, so sometimes you have to kind of get their attention or they won’t hear you, especially down by the back hallway where the sound bounces off the tile—

I don’t sit down and make a plan for it like some people do.\n\nSometimes if the day changes, like there’s an event or somebody needs a room turned over, I kind of just start with whatever seems most urgent. Which… now that I say it, maybe that is organizing, I’m not sure. At church they’ll have these setup lists sometimes, and the folding tables all have to face a certain way, and there’s always that one closet where the chairs get jammed in wrong—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed daily organizing as a big problem, but I might not be thinking of it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main things that brings me in. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like in a staff meeting or when they’re going over a church event setup, I feel like there’s a motor humming under my skin or something.\n\nI’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or I’ll find a reason to go check on something, move a chair, refill coffee, whatever. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just being useful, but really I think I just need to move. Those briefings can go on, and the chairs at church are those metal folding ones, the kind that pinch if you sit wrong—\n\nBut yeah, sitting still or standing still, I get restless pretty quick.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: Strong mood swings… I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something’s not where it’s supposed to be, or if people leave a mess right after I cleaned it — which happens more than you’d think, especially near the cafeteria area, because folks will just drop napkins and not even see them—\n\nSorry. Mood swings, though… I’m not sure I’d say strong ones. I haven’t really noticed big ups and downs like that. Maybe you’d have to explain what kind you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I notice things around me, especially at work — like if a light’s flickering or someone left a cart in the wrong hallway, I’ll see it. But that’s sort of part of my job, too.\n\nIf I’m cleaning and somebody walks by or there’s a noise, I might look over, but I don’t know if I’d call it distracted. The other day there was this squeaky door near the supply closet, and once you hear it you can’t unhear it, you know? I kept thinking someone should oil that hinge, and then I was looking for the oil can, except I think it got moved after the last maintenance guy used it—\n\nSorry. Your question was whether I’m easily distracted. I guess I haven’t really kept track of that. I don’t know that I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, like inserting yourself into their conversations or games?\n\nPatient: Physically intrude… like jumping into people’s conversations or games? I don’t know that I do that a lot. I mean, sometimes if I’m nearby and I hear someone talking about something in the building — like a spill or a room needing chairs — I might step in because I think I can help.\n\nBut if you mean like barging in or getting in people’s space, I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t play games much, unless you count the church picnic stuff, and that’s usually me hauling tables around or making sure the trash bags don’t tear, because those cheap ones split right down the side—\n\nSorry. I guess my answer is I’m not sure that’s something I do, at least not that I’ve been told.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers to questions before the person has finished asking them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do that more than I should. Like if my supervisor starts telling me something, or one of the church staff says, “Can you go check—” I’ll jump in and say, “The bathrooms?” or “The fellowship hall?” before they even finish.\n\nSometimes I’m right, because it’s usually the same kinds of things, you know — supplies, trash, a spill, chairs needing to be moved. My brain just kind of gets ahead of their sentence.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're in the middle of doing something, like a task or a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, if somebody’s in the middle of something and I need to ask where a mop bucket went, I might step in for a second, but I don’t know if that counts as interrupting.\n\nAt work people are always doing something, so it’s kind of hard to tell. That floor machine’s loud too, so sometimes you have to kind of get their attention or they won’t hear you, especially down by the back hallway where the sound bounces off the tile—\n\nBut, um… I haven’t really noticed that I’m always interrupting people’s projects or anything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. Waiting my turn in a conversation is hard if I already know what I want to say. I get kind of antsy, like I’m holding it in, and then I’ll jump in too early.

It’s sort of the same feeling at work when I’m waiting on the freight elevator, or the supply closet key, or somebody to decide which room needs cleaning next. I’ll start pacing or pushing like, “Okay, can we get moving?” even when it’s not really my turn yet. Conversations can feel like that too — like I’m waiting at a stoplight that’s taking too long.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the main thing is I can’t seem to sit still when I’m supposed to. It’s like there’s this little motor running under my skin, you know? Like at staff meetings at the building, or even church event briefings — and those can go on longer than they need to, honestly, especially when folks start talking about where to put the folding tables — but anyway, I’ll be sitting there shifting around, then I’m standing, or I’m finding some reason to go check something or move around. It’s getting noticeable, and I figured I should talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early. In grade school I was always getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, talking over the teacher before I even realized I was doing it, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd directions — if the teacher was explaining an assignment, I’d already be halfway into it before she finished, then I’d have to redo it. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” which I guess was her way of saying I couldn’t just settle down like the other kids. She said that a lot, actually.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably. Like if my supervisor starts telling me what needs doing in the building, I’ll jump in before he’s finished because I think I already know where he’s going with it. Same thing with church staff when they’re giving instructions for an event.\n\nAnd sometimes I’m right, but sometimes I’m not, and then they have to stop and correct me. Which is embarrassing, you know? I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve waited for the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like blurting out answers or doing things without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that. Mostly it’s with instructions — like my supervisor will start telling me what needs to be done, or someone at church is explaining how they want something set up, and I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they’ve finished.\n\nSometimes I’ve got it right, but other times I’m off, and then they have to stop and correct me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. It’s just like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one exactly. I mean, I finish my work, generally… at least I think I do. If someone gives me a list, I’ll usually get through it.\n\nSometimes people say “follow through” and I start thinking about, like, big projects, and I don’t really have many of those at work — it’s more cleaning rooms, checking bathrooms, taking trash out, floors, that kind of thing. Actually the floor buffer, that thing can be touchy if it’s not sitting right, and we had one at the school years ago that would pull hard to the left—\n\nSorry, what I mean is… I haven’t really noticed not finishing tasks as a big problem. I may need you to be more specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it organizing, exactly. My day’s pretty routine, you know — trash, bathrooms, floors, supplies, that sort of thing. I don’t sit down and make a plan for it like some people do.\n\nSometimes if the day changes, like there’s an event or somebody needs a room turned over, I kind of just start with whatever seems most urgent. Which… now that I say it, maybe that is organizing, I’m not sure. At church they’ll have these setup lists sometimes, and the folding tables all have to face a certain way, and there’s always that one closet where the chairs get jammed in wrong—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed daily organizing as a big problem, but I might not be thinking of it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main things that brings me in. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like in a staff meeting or when they’re going over a church event setup, I feel like there’s a motor humming under my skin or something.\n\nI’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or I’ll find a reason to go check on something, move a chair, refill coffee, whatever. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just being useful, but really I think I just need to move. Those briefings can go on, and the chairs at church are those metal folding ones, the kind that pinch if you sit wrong—\n\nBut yeah, sitting still or standing still, I get restless pretty quick.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: Strong mood swings… I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something’s not where it’s supposed to be, or if people leave a mess right after I cleaned it — which happens more than you’d think, especially near the cafeteria area, because folks will just drop napkins and not even see them—\n\nSorry. Mood swings, though… I’m not sure I’d say strong ones. I haven’t really noticed big ups and downs like that. Maybe you’d have to explain what kind you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I notice things around me, especially at work — like if a light’s flickering or someone left a cart in the wrong hallway, I’ll see it. But that’s sort of part of my job, too.\n\nIf I’m cleaning and somebody walks by or there’s a noise, I might look over, but I don’t know if I’d call it distracted. The other day there was this squeaky door near the supply closet, and once you hear it you can’t unhear it, you know? I kept thinking someone should oil that hinge, and then I was looking for the oil can, except I think it got moved after the last maintenance guy used it—\n\nSorry. Your question was whether I’m easily distracted. I guess I haven’t really kept track of that. I don’t know that I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, like inserting yourself into their conversations or games?\n\nPatient: Physically intrude… like jumping into people’s conversations or games? I don’t know that I do that a lot. I mean, sometimes if I’m nearby and I hear someone talking about something in the building — like a spill or a room needing chairs — I might step in because I think I can help.\n\nBut if you mean like barging in or getting in people’s space, I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t play games much, unless you count the church picnic stuff, and that’s usually me hauling tables around or making sure the trash bags don’t tear, because those cheap ones split right down the side—\n\nSorry. I guess my answer is I’m not sure that’s something I do, at least not that I’ve been told.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers to questions before the person has finished asking them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do that more than I should. Like if my supervisor starts telling me something, or one of the church staff says, “Can you go check—” I’ll jump in and say, “The bathrooms?” or “The fellowship hall?” before they even finish.\n\nSometimes I’m right, because it’s usually the same kinds of things, you know — supplies, trash, a spill, chairs needing to be moved. But sometimes I guess wrong and they have to stop me and say, “No, let me finish,” which is… a little embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut them off. My brain just kind of gets ahead of their sentence.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're in the middle of doing something, like a task or a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, if somebody’s in the middle of something and I need to ask where a mop bucket went, I might step in for a second, but I don’t know if that counts as interrupting.\n\nAt work people are always doing something, so it’s kind of hard to tell. Like if maintenance is working on a door and I need to get through with the floor machine, I’ll ask, or I’ll wave at them. That floor machine’s loud too, so sometimes you have to kind of get their attention or they won’t hear you, especially down by the back hallway where the sound bounces off the tile—\n\nBut, um… I haven’t really noticed that I’m always interrupting people’s projects or anything. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Waiting my turn in a conversation is hard if I already know what I want to say. I get kind of antsy, like I’m holding it in, and then I’ll jump in too early.\n\nIt’s sort of the same feeling at work when I’m waiting on the freight elevator, or the supply closet key, or somebody to decide which room needs cleaning next. I’ll start pacing or pushing like, “Okay, can we get moving?” even when it’s not really my turn yet. Conversations can feel like that too — like I’m waiting at a stoplight that’s taking too long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I get kind of antsy, like I’m holding it in, and then I’ll jump in too early.\n\nIt’s sort of the same feeling at work when I’m waiting on the freight elevator, or the supply closet key, or somebody to decide which room needs cleaning next. Conversations can feel like that too — like I’m waiting at a stoplight that’s taking too long.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often have difficulty paying attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 56 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… paying attention to details? I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.

At work there’s a lot of little stuff, like making sure the trash bags are tucked in right or the soap dispensers aren’t empty, and sometimes the little paper towel bits hide under the sinks—those bathrooms at the church, the lighting in one of them is kind of dim, so you can miss things if you don’t bend down and really look. But I don’t know if that’s me not paying attention or just the way the room is.

I mean, I try to do a good job. If somebody says I missed something, I’ll go back and fix it. But I can’t really say whether it happens more than normal. What kind of details do you mean?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the main thing is I can’t seem to sit still when I’m supposed to. It’s like there’s this little motor running under my skin, you know? Like at staff meetings at the building, or even church event briefings — and those can go on longer than they need to, honestly, especially when folks start talking about where to put the folding tables — but anyway, I’ll be sitting there shifting around, then I’m standing, or I’m finding some reason to go check something or move around. It’s getting noticeable, and I figured I should talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early. In grade school I was always getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, talking over the teacher before I even realized I was doing it, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd directions — if the teacher was explaining an assignment, I’d already be halfway into it before she finished, then I’d have to redo it. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” which I guess was her way of saying I couldn’t just settle down like the other kids. She said that a lot, actually.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably. Like if my supervisor starts telling me what needs doing in the building, I’ll jump in before he’s finished because I think I already know where he’s going with it. Same thing with church staff when they’re giving instructions for an event.\n\nAnd sometimes I’m right, but sometimes I’m not, and then they have to stop and correct me. Which is embarrassing, you know? I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve waited for the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like blurting out answers or doing things without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that. Mostly it’s with instructions — like my supervisor will start telling me what needs to be done, or someone at church is explaining how they want something set up, and I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they’ve finished.\n\nSometimes I’ve got it right, but other times I’m off, and then they have to stop and correct me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. It’s just like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one exactly. I mean, I finish my work, generally… at least I think I do. If someone gives me a list, I’ll usually get through it.\n\nSometimes people say “follow through” and I start thinking about, like, big projects, and I don’t really have many of those at work — it’s more cleaning rooms, checking bathrooms, taking trash out, floors, that kind of thing. Actually the floor buffer, that thing can be touchy if it’s not sitting right, and we had one at the school years ago that would pull hard to the left—\n\nSorry, what I mean is… I haven’t really noticed not finishing tasks as a big problem. I may need you to be more specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it organizing, exactly. My day’s pretty routine, you know — trash, bathrooms, floors, supplies, that sort of thing. I don’t sit down and make a plan for it like some people do.\n\nSometimes if the day changes, like there’s an event or somebody needs a room turned over, I kind of just start with whatever seems most urgent. Which… now that I say it, maybe that is organizing, I’m not sure. At church they’ll have these setup lists sometimes, and the folding tables all have to face a certain way, and there’s always that one closet where the chairs get jammed in wrong—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed daily organizing as a big problem, but I might not be thinking of it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main things that brings me in. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like in a staff meeting or when they’re going over a church event setup, I feel like there’s a motor humming under my skin or something.\n\nI’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or I’ll find a reason to go check on something, move a chair, refill coffee, whatever. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just being useful, but really I think I just need to move. Those briefings can go on, and the chairs at church are those metal folding ones, the kind that pinch if you sit wrong—\n\nBut yeah, sitting still or standing still, I get restless pretty quick.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: Strong mood swings… I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something’s not where it’s supposed to be, or if people leave a mess right after I cleaned it — which happens more than you’d think, especially near the cafeteria area, because folks will just drop napkins and not even see them—\n\nSorry. Mood swings, though… I’m not sure I’d say strong ones. I haven’t really noticed big ups and downs like that. Maybe you’d have to explain what kind you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I notice things around me, especially at work — like if a light’s flickering or someone left a cart in the wrong hallway, I’ll see it. But that’s sort of part of my job, too.\n\nIf I’m cleaning and somebody walks by or there’s a noise, I might look over, but I don’t know if I’d call it distracted. The other day there was this squeaky door near the supply closet, and once you hear it you can’t unhear it, you know? I kept thinking someone should oil that hinge, and then I was looking for the oil can, except I think it got moved after the last maintenance guy used it—\n\nSorry. Your question was whether I’m easily distracted. I guess I haven’t really kept track of that. I don’t know that I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, like inserting yourself into their conversations or games?\n\nPatient: Physically intrude… like jumping into people’s conversations or games? I don’t know that I do that a lot. I mean, sometimes if I’m nearby and I hear someone talking about something in the building — like a spill or a room needing chairs — I might step in because I think I can help.\n\nBut if you mean like barging in or getting in people’s space, I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t play games much, unless you count the church picnic stuff, and that’s usually me hauling tables around or making sure the trash bags don’t tear, because those cheap ones split right down the side—\n\nSorry. I guess my answer is I’m not sure that’s something I do, at least not that I’ve been told.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers to questions before the person has finished asking them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do that more than I should. Like if my supervisor starts telling me something, or one of the church staff says, “Can you go check—” I’ll jump in and say, “The bathrooms?” or “The fellowship hall?” before they even finish.\n\nSometimes I’m right, because it’s usually the same kinds of things, you know — supplies, trash, a spill, chairs needing to be moved. But sometimes I guess wrong and they have to stop me and say, “No, let me finish,” which is… a little embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut them off. My brain just kind of gets ahead of their sentence.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're in the middle of doing something, like a task or a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, if somebody’s in the middle of something and I need to ask where a mop bucket went, I might step in for a second, but I don’t know if that counts as interrupting.\n\nAt work people are always doing something, so it’s kind of hard to tell. Like if maintenance is working on a door and I need to get through with the floor machine, I’ll ask, or I’ll wave at them. That floor machine’s loud too, so sometimes you have to kind of get their attention or they won’t hear you, especially down by the back hallway where the sound bounces off the tile—\n\nBut, um… I haven’t really noticed that I’m always interrupting people’s projects or anything. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Waiting my turn in a conversation is hard if I already know what I want to say. I get kind of antsy, like I’m holding it in, and then I’ll jump in too early.\n\nIt’s sort of the same feeling at work when I’m waiting on the freight elevator, or the supply closet key, or somebody to decide which room needs cleaning next. I’ll start pacing or pushing like, “Okay, can we get moving?” even when it’s not really my turn yet. Conversations can feel like that too — like I’m waiting at a stoplight that’s taking too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Um… paying attention to details? I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nAt work there’s a lot of little stuff, like making sure the trash bags are tucked in right or the soap dispensers aren’t empty, and sometimes the little paper towel bits hide under the sinks—those bathrooms at the church, the lighting in one of them is kind of dim, so you can miss things if you don’t bend down and really look. But I don’t know if that’s me not paying attention or just the way the room is.\n\nI mean, I try to do a good job. If somebody says I missed something, I’ll go back and fix it. But I can’t really say whether it happens more than normal. What kind of details do you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Waiting my turn in a conversation is hard if I already know what I want to say. I get kind of antsy, like I’m holding it in, and then I’ll jump in too early.\n\nIt’s sort of the same feeling at work when I’m waiting on the freight elevator, or the supply closet key, or somebody to decide which room needs cleaning next. I’ll start pacing or pushing like, “Okay, can we get moving?” even when it’s not really my turn yet. Conversations can feel like that too — like I’m waiting at a stoplight that’s taking too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Um… paying attention to details? I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nAt work there’s a lot of little stuff, like making sure the trash bags are tucked in right or the soap dispensers aren’t empty, and sometimes the little paper towel bits hide under the sinks—those bathrooms at the church, the lighting in one of them is kind of dim, so you can miss things if you don’t bend down and really look. But I don’t know if that’s me not paying attention or just the way the room is.\n\nI mean, I try to do a good job. If somebody says I missed something, I’ll go back and fix it. But I can’t really say whether it happens more than normal. What kind of details do you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often lose things that you need for work or daily activities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 15\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question asks about losing necessary items for work or daily activities, which is an inattentive-type symptom not covered by the provided symptom profile items."}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I lose things a lot. I mean, everybody misplaces stuff now and then, right?

At work I try to keep things in the same places, like keys on the ring or supplies on the cart, because if you set something down in one closet and then go answer somebody calling you from the hallway, it can turn into a whole thing. Those storage closets all kind of look the same too, especially the ones near the fellowship hall—one has the light that flickers, and I keep saying somebody should replace that ballast, but then you need the ladder, and the ladder’s usually in the other room—

But, sorry, what was the question? Losing things? I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed it being a big problem, but I do like having a regular spot for things so I don’t have to go hunting.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the main thing is I can’t seem to sit still when I’m supposed to. It’s like there’s this little motor running under my skin, you know? Like at staff meetings at the building, or even church event briefings — and those can go on longer than they need to, honestly, especially when folks start talking about where to put the folding tables — but anyway, I’ll be sitting there shifting around, then I’m standing, or I’m finding some reason to go check something or move around. It’s getting noticeable, and I figured I should talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early. In grade school I was always getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, talking over the teacher before I even realized I was doing it, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd directions — if the teacher was explaining an assignment, I’d already be halfway into it before she finished, then I’d have to redo it. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” which I guess was her way of saying I couldn’t just settle down like the other kids. She said that a lot, actually.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably. Like if my supervisor starts telling me what needs doing in the building, I’ll jump in before he’s finished because I think I already know where he’s going with it. Same thing with church staff when they’re giving instructions for an event.\n\nAnd sometimes I’m right, but sometimes I’m not, and then they have to stop and correct me. Which is embarrassing, you know? I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve waited for the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like blurting out answers or doing things without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that. Mostly it’s with instructions — like my supervisor will start telling me what needs to be done, or someone at church is explaining how they want something set up, and I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they’ve finished.\n\nSometimes I’ve got it right, but other times I’m off, and then they have to stop and correct me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. It’s just like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one exactly. I mean, I finish my work, generally… at least I think I do. If someone gives me a list, I’ll usually get through it.\n\nSometimes people say “follow through” and I start thinking about, like, big projects, and I don’t really have many of those at work — it’s more cleaning rooms, checking bathrooms, taking trash out, floors, that kind of thing. Actually the floor buffer, that thing can be touchy if it’s not sitting right, and we had one at the school years ago that would pull hard to the left—\n\nSorry, what I mean is… I haven’t really noticed not finishing tasks as a big problem. I may need you to be more specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it organizing, exactly. My day’s pretty routine, you know — trash, bathrooms, floors, supplies, that sort of thing. I don’t sit down and make a plan for it like some people do.\n\nSometimes if the day changes, like there’s an event or somebody needs a room turned over, I kind of just start with whatever seems most urgent. Which… now that I say it, maybe that is organizing, I’m not sure. At church they’ll have these setup lists sometimes, and the folding tables all have to face a certain way, and there’s always that one closet where the chairs get jammed in wrong—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed daily organizing as a big problem, but I might not be thinking of it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main things that brings me in. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like in a staff meeting or when they’re going over a church event setup, I feel like there’s a motor humming under my skin or something.\n\nI’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or I’ll find a reason to go check on something, move a chair, refill coffee, whatever. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just being useful, but really I think I just need to move. Those briefings can go on, and the chairs at church are those metal folding ones, the kind that pinch if you sit wrong—\n\nBut yeah, sitting still or standing still, I get restless pretty quick.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: Strong mood swings… I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something’s not where it’s supposed to be, or if people leave a mess right after I cleaned it — which happens more than you’d think, especially near the cafeteria area, because folks will just drop napkins and not even see them—\n\nSorry. Mood swings, though… I’m not sure I’d say strong ones. I haven’t really noticed big ups and downs like that. Maybe you’d have to explain what kind you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I notice things around me, especially at work — like if a light’s flickering or someone left a cart in the wrong hallway, I’ll see it. But that’s sort of part of my job, too.\n\nIf I’m cleaning and somebody walks by or there’s a noise, I might look over, but I don’t know if I’d call it distracted. The other day there was this squeaky door near the supply closet, and once you hear it you can’t unhear it, you know? I kept thinking someone should oil that hinge, and then I was looking for the oil can, except I think it got moved after the last maintenance guy used it—\n\nSorry. Your question was whether I’m easily distracted. I guess I haven’t really kept track of that. I don’t know that I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, like inserting yourself into their conversations or games?\n\nPatient: Physically intrude… like jumping into people’s conversations or games? I don’t know that I do that a lot. I mean, sometimes if I’m nearby and I hear someone talking about something in the building — like a spill or a room needing chairs — I might step in because I think I can help.\n\nBut if you mean like barging in or getting in people’s space, I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t play games much, unless you count the church picnic stuff, and that’s usually me hauling tables around or making sure the trash bags don’t tear, because those cheap ones split right down the side—\n\nSorry. I guess my answer is I’m not sure that’s something I do, at least not that I’ve been told.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers to questions before the person has finished asking them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do that more than I should. Like if my supervisor starts telling me something, or one of the church staff says, “Can you go check—” I’ll jump in and say, “The bathrooms?” or “The fellowship hall?” before they even finish.\n\nSometimes I’m right, because it’s usually the same kinds of things, you know — supplies, trash, a spill, chairs needing to be moved. But sometimes I guess wrong and they have to stop me and say, “No, let me finish,” which is… a little embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut them off. My brain just kind of gets ahead of their sentence.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're in the middle of doing something, like a task or a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, if somebody’s in the middle of something and I need to ask where a mop bucket went, I might step in for a second, but I don’t know if that counts as interrupting.\n\nAt work people are always doing something, so it’s kind of hard to tell. Like if maintenance is working on a door and I need to get through with the floor machine, I’ll ask, or I’ll wave at them. That floor machine’s loud too, so sometimes you have to kind of get their attention or they won’t hear you, especially down by the back hallway where the sound bounces off the tile—\n\nBut, um… I haven’t really noticed that I’m always interrupting people’s projects or anything. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Waiting my turn in a conversation is hard if I already know what I want to say. I get kind of antsy, like I’m holding it in, and then I’ll jump in too early.\n\nIt’s sort of the same feeling at work when I’m waiting on the freight elevator, or the supply closet key, or somebody to decide which room needs cleaning next. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the main thing is I can’t seem to sit still when I’m supposed to. It’s like there’s this little motor running under my skin, you know? Like at staff meetings at the building, or even church event briefings — and those can go on longer than they need to, honestly, especially when folks start talking about where to put the folding tables — but anyway, I’ll be sitting there shifting around, then I’m standing, or I’m finding some reason to go check something or move around. It’s getting noticeable, and I figured I should talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early. In grade school I was always getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, talking over the teacher before I even realized I was doing it, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd directions — if the teacher was explaining an assignment, I’d already be halfway into it before she finished, then I’d have to redo it. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” which I guess was her way of saying I couldn’t just settle down like the other kids. She said that a lot, actually.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I’d like to admit, probably. Like if my supervisor starts telling me what needs doing in the building, I’ll jump in before he’s finished because I think I already know where he’s going with it. Same thing with church staff when they’re giving instructions for an event.\n\nAnd sometimes I’m right, but sometimes I’m not, and then they have to stop and correct me. Which is embarrassing, you know? I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve waited for the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking, like blurting out answers or doing things without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that. Mostly it’s with instructions — like my supervisor will start telling me what needs to be done, or someone at church is explaining how they want something set up, and I’ll jump in with what I think they’re about to say before they’ve finished.\n\nSometimes I’ve got it right, but other times I’m off, and then they have to stop and correct me. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. It’s just like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one exactly. I mean, I finish my work, generally… at least I think I do. If someone gives me a list, I’ll usually get through it.\n\nSometimes people say “follow through” and I start thinking about, like, big projects, and I don’t really have many of those at work — it’s more cleaning rooms, checking bathrooms, taking trash out, floors, that kind of thing. Actually the floor buffer, that thing can be touchy if it’s not sitting right, and we had one at the school years ago that would pull hard to the left—\n\nSorry, what I mean is… I haven’t really noticed not finishing tasks as a big problem. I may need you to be more specific.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your daily activities or tasks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it organizing, exactly. My day’s pretty routine, you know — trash, bathrooms, floors, supplies, that sort of thing. I don’t sit down and make a plan for it like some people do.\n\nSometimes if the day changes, like there’s an event or somebody needs a room turned over, I kind of just start with whatever seems most urgent. Which… now that I say it, maybe that is organizing, I’m not sure. At church they’ll have these setup lists sometimes, and the folding tables all have to face a certain way, and there’s always that one closet where the chairs get jammed in wrong—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed daily organizing as a big problem, but I might not be thinking of it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main things that brings me in. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like in a staff meeting or when they’re going over a church event setup, I feel like there’s a motor humming under my skin or something.\n\nI’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or I’ll find a reason to go check on something, move a chair, refill coffee, whatever. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just being useful, but really I think I just need to move. Those briefings can go on, and the chairs at church are those metal folding ones, the kind that pinch if you sit wrong—\n\nBut yeah, sitting still or standing still, I get restless pretty quick.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: Strong mood swings… I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something’s not where it’s supposed to be, or if people leave a mess right after I cleaned it — which happens more than you’d think, especially near the cafeteria area, because folks will just drop napkins and not even see them—\n\nSorry. Mood swings, though… I’m not sure I’d say strong ones. I haven’t really noticed big ups and downs like that. Maybe you’d have to explain what kind you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I notice things around me, especially at work — like if a light’s flickering or someone left a cart in the wrong hallway, I’ll see it. But that’s sort of part of my job, too.\n\nIf I’m cleaning and somebody walks by or there’s a noise, I might look over, but I don’t know if I’d call it distracted. The other day there was this squeaky door near the supply closet, and once you hear it you can’t unhear it, you know? I kept thinking someone should oil that hinge, and then I was looking for the oil can, except I think it got moved after the last maintenance guy used it—\n\nSorry. Your question was whether I’m easily distracted. I guess I haven’t really kept track of that. I don’t know that I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, like inserting yourself into their conversations or games?\n\nPatient: Physically intrude… like jumping into people’s conversations or games? I don’t know that I do that a lot. I mean, sometimes if I’m nearby and I hear someone talking about something in the building — like a spill or a room needing chairs — I might step in because I think I can help.\n\nBut if you mean like barging in or getting in people’s space, I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t play games much, unless you count the church picnic stuff, and that’s usually me hauling tables around or making sure the trash bags don’t tear, because those cheap ones split right down the side—\n\nSorry. I guess my answer is I’m not sure that’s something I do, at least not that I’ve been told.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers to questions before the person has finished asking them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do that more than I should. Like if my supervisor starts telling me something, or one of the church staff says, “Can you go check—” I’ll jump in and say, “The bathrooms?” or “The fellowship hall?” before they even finish.\n\nSometimes I’m right, because it’s usually the same kinds of things, you know — supplies, trash, a spill, chairs needing to be moved. But sometimes I guess wrong and they have to stop me and say, “No, let me finish,” which is… a little embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut them off. My brain just kind of gets ahead of their sentence.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're in the middle of doing something, like a task or a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, if somebody’s in the middle of something and I need to ask where a mop bucket went, I might step in for a second, but I don’t know if that counts as interrupting.\n\nAt work people are always doing something, so it’s kind of hard to tell. Like if maintenance is working on a door and I need to get through with the floor machine, I’ll ask, or I’ll wave at them. That floor machine’s loud too, so sometimes you have to kind of get their attention or they won’t hear you, especially down by the back hallway where the sound bounces off the tile—\n\nBut, um… I haven’t really noticed that I’m always interrupting people’s projects or anything. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Waiting my turn in a conversation is hard if I already know what I want to say. I get kind of antsy, like I’m holding it in, and then I’ll jump in too early.\n\nIt’s sort of the same feeling at work when I’m waiting on the freight elevator, or the supply closet key, or somebody to decide which room needs cleaning next. I’ll start pacing or pushing like, “Okay, can we get moving?” even when it’s not really my turn yet. Conversations can feel like that too — like I’m waiting at a stoplight that’s taking too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Um… paying attention to details? I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nAt work there’s a lot of little stuff, like making sure the trash bags are tucked in right or the soap dispensers aren’t empty, and sometimes the little paper towel bits hide under the sinks—those bathrooms at the church, the lighting in one of them is kind of dim, so you can miss things if you don’t bend down and really look. But I don’t know if that’s me not paying attention or just the way the room is.\n\nI mean, I try to do a good job. If somebody says I missed something, I’ll go back and fix it. But I can’t really say whether it happens more than normal. I mean, everybody misplaces stuff now and then, right?\n\nAt work I try to keep things in the same places, like keys on the ring or supplies on the cart, because if you set something down in one closet and then go answer somebody calling you from the hallway, it can turn into a whole thing. Those storage closets all kind of look the same too, especially the ones near the fellowship hall—one has the light that flickers, and I keep saying somebody should replace that ballast, but then you need the ladder, and the ladder’s usually in the other room—\n\nBut, sorry, what was the question? Losing things? I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed it being a big problem, but I do like having a regular spot for things so I don’t have to go hunting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention or sitting still and controlling your behavior?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the sitting still and controlling myself part. The attention thing… I mean, maybe sometimes, but what really bothers me is I feel like there’s this motor humming under my skin when I’m supposed to stay put.\n\nLike in staff meetings, or when they’re giving instructions before a church event, and everybody’s just sitting there listening, I’m shifting around, leaning forward, standing up if I can get away with it. Yeah… yeah, that part goes way back. I remember in grade school getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, like I’d just be halfway standing before I even realized I was doing it.

At church they’ll have these setup lists sometimes, and the folding tables all have to face a certain way, and there’s always that one closet where the chairs get jammed in wrong—\n\nSorry. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed daily organizing as a big problem, but I might not be thinking of it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually one of the main things that brings me in. If I’m supposed to just sit still, like in a staff meeting or when they’re going over a church event setup, I feel like there’s a motor humming under my skin or something.\n\nI’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or I’ll find a reason to go check on something, move a chair, refill coffee, whatever. Sometimes I tell myself I’m just being useful, but really I think I just need to move. Those briefings can go on, and the chairs at church are those metal folding ones, the kind that pinch if you sit wrong—\n\nBut yeah, sitting still or standing still, I get restless pretty quick.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong mood swings?\n\nPatient: Strong mood swings… I don’t know. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something’s not where it’s supposed to be, or if people leave a mess right after I cleaned it — which happens more than you’d think, especially near the cafeteria area, because folks will just drop napkins and not even see them—\n\nSorry. Mood swings, though… I’m not sure I’d say strong ones. I haven’t really noticed big ups and downs like that. Maybe you’d have to explain what kind you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I notice things around me, especially at work — like if a light’s flickering or someone left a cart in the wrong hallway, I’ll see it. But that’s sort of part of my job, too.\n\nIf I’m cleaning and somebody walks by or there’s a noise, I might look over, but I don’t know if I’d call it distracted. The other day there was this squeaky door near the supply closet, and once you hear it you can’t unhear it, you know? I kept thinking someone should oil that hinge, and then I was looking for the oil can, except I think it got moved after the last maintenance guy used it—\n\nSorry. Your question was whether I’m easily distracted. I guess I haven’t really kept track of that. I don’t know that I’d say it’s a big thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often intrude on others physically, like inserting yourself into their conversations or games?\n\nPatient: Physically intrude… like jumping into people’s conversations or games? I don’t know that I do that a lot. I mean, sometimes if I’m nearby and I hear someone talking about something in the building — like a spill or a room needing chairs — I might step in because I think I can help.\n\nBut if you mean like barging in or getting in people’s space, I haven’t really noticed that. I don’t play games much, unless you count the church picnic stuff, and that’s usually me hauling tables around or making sure the trash bags don’t tear, because those cheap ones split right down the side—\n\nSorry. I guess my answer is I’m not sure that’s something I do, at least not that I’ve been told.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers to questions before the person has finished asking them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I probably do that more than I should. Like if my supervisor starts telling me something, or one of the church staff says, “Can you go check—” I’ll jump in and say, “The bathrooms?” or “The fellowship hall?” before they even finish.\n\nSometimes I’m right, because it’s usually the same kinds of things, you know — supplies, trash, a spill, chairs needing to be moved. But sometimes I guess wrong and they have to stop me and say, “No, let me finish,” which is… a little embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut them off. My brain just kind of gets ahead of their sentence.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're in the middle of doing something, like a task or a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, if somebody’s in the middle of something and I need to ask where a mop bucket went, I might step in for a second, but I don’t know if that counts as interrupting.\n\nAt work people are always doing something, so it’s kind of hard to tell. Like if maintenance is working on a door and I need to get through with the floor machine, I’ll ask, or I’ll wave at them. That floor machine’s loud too, so sometimes you have to kind of get their attention or they won’t hear you, especially down by the back hallway where the sound bounces off the tile—\n\nBut, um… I haven’t really noticed that I’m always interrupting people’s projects or anything. I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Waiting my turn in a conversation is hard if I already know what I want to say. I get kind of antsy, like I’m holding it in, and then I’ll jump in too early.\n\nIt’s sort of the same feeling at work when I’m waiting on the freight elevator, or the supply closet key, or somebody to decide which room needs cleaning next. I’ll start pacing or pushing like, “Okay, can we get moving?” even when it’s not really my turn yet. Conversations can feel like that too — like I’m waiting at a stoplight that’s taking too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Um… paying attention to details? I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nAt work there’s a lot of little stuff, like making sure the trash bags are tucked in right or the soap dispensers aren’t empty, and sometimes the little paper towel bits hide under the sinks—those bathrooms at the church, the lighting in one of them is kind of dim, so you can miss things if you don’t bend down and really look. But I don’t know if that’s me not paying attention or just the way the room is.\n\nI mean, I try to do a good job. If somebody says I missed something, I’ll go back and fix it. But I can’t really say whether it happens more than normal. What kind of details do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things that you need for work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I lose things a lot. I mean, everybody misplaces stuff now and then, right?\n\nAt work I try to keep things in the same places, like keys on the ring or supplies on the cart, because if you set something down in one closet and then go answer somebody calling you from the hallway, it can turn into a whole thing. Those storage closets all kind of look the same too, especially the ones near the fellowship hall—one has the light that flickers, and I keep saying somebody should replace that ballast, but then you need the ladder, and the ladder’s usually in the other room—\n\nBut, sorry, what was the question? Losing things? I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed it being a big problem, but I do like having a regular spot for things so I don’t have to go hunting.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is paying attention or sitting still and controlling your behavior?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the sitting still and controlling myself part. The attention thing… I mean, maybe sometimes, but what really bothers me is I feel like there’s this motor humming under my skin when I’m supposed to stay put.\n\nLike in staff meetings, or when they’re giving instructions before a church event, and everybody’s just sitting there listening, I’m shifting around, leaning forward, standing up if I can get away with it. Sometimes I’ll make an excuse to go check something or move a cart or get supplies, just so I’m not stuck in the chair. It’s like my body wants to go before the meeting is done.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity for as long as you can remember, even as a young child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that part goes way back. I remember in grade school getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, like I’d just be halfway standing before I even realized I was doing it.\n\nAnd talking over the teacher too—she’d be explaining something and I’d already be blurting out or trying to start before she finished the directions. I got told to “wait” a lot. My mother used to say I was wound too tight, even when I was little.\n\nI mean, I don’t remember every single year or anything, but that feeling of needing to move and jumping ahead… that’s not new.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
